# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 per stationary robot each step → robots should MOVE
- After switch time: score += 1 per moving robot each step → robots should STOP
- Switch signal: a wall block lights up GREEN
- Floor is uniformly GRAY throughout

**Optimal behavior:**
1. Before switch: all robots move (explore)
2. After switch: all robots stop
3. The GREEN wall light is the only detectable cue for the switch

**Key perception:** C=7 (perceive green color, color id=2) is the trigger to switch from moving to stopping.

**Champion analysis:** Score 10029.90. The champion uses Repulsion (s0), Stop (s1), Stop (s2) with complex transitions. It's not optimal because it doesn't use the green light perception directly.

**Better design:**
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when green light perceived
- State 1: Stop → maybe transition back if no green seen (with low prob)

The ideal: Move before switch, detect green, stop permanently.

**Machines to generate:**

1. **Survival:** Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation variants:** Tweak champion parameters
3. **Exploration:** New topologies using GoToColor(green)/perceive green condition

Let me design 14 machines:

- 2 states: Explore → Stop on green perception
- 3 states: Explore → Stop on green, with attraction phase
- Variants with different transition probabilities

FSM designs:

**Simple 2-state:** S0=Exploration, S1=Stop. S0→S1 on C=7 (green, p=1.0). S1→S0 on C=5 (low prob, so mostly stays stopped).

**3-state with attraction:** S0=Explore, S1=Attraction (cluster), S2=Stop. Green triggers stop.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:** Score 7887.98. The champion is elegant:
- S0: GoToColor(green) → moves toward green wall when seen
- S0→S1: when perceives green (C=7, color=2, p=1.0) → transition to Stop
- S1: Stop → with tiny probability (0.001) transitions back to S0

This is nearly optimal! Before switch: robots move (GoToColor green, but green not visible yet, so they still move/explore). After switch: green appears, robots perceive it, transition to Stop. The issue: GoToColor without a visible target may behave oddly (might stop or spin). Also the transition fires immediately when green is seen, so robots stop.

**Why score isn't perfect (~7887 instead of ~0):**
- Before switch: if robots are stationary (GoToColor with no target visible might stop them), score accumulates
- After switch: some robots may not see green quickly enough

**Improvements to explore:**
1. Replace S0 with Exploration (B=0) so robots definitely move before switch, then transition to Stop on green
2. Add a second transition from S0 to handle the case where green isn't seen yet
3. Try having S0=Exploration with transition to Stop on green perception
4. Keep champion exactly
5. Try 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop, with green perception triggers
6. Vary the probability in the green-detection transition

The key insight: GoToColor(green) when green isn't visible may cause robots to stop (no target), scoring badly before switch. Using Exploration (B=0) ensures movement before switch.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:** Score 6191.98.
- S0: GoToColor(green) with two transitions:
  - On perceiving green (C=7, p=1.0): stay in S0 (self-loop back... wait, destination 0 from state 0 means the only OTHER state, which is S1)
  - Actually with 2 states, from state 0, D=0 means state 1. So both transitions go to S1.
  - Wait: `--n0x0 0` means D=0 → the other state (state 1). `--n0x1 0` also → state 1.
  - S0→S1 when perceives green (p=1.0) OR with tiny probability (0.001)
- S1: Stop → with p=0.0005 transitions back to S0

**Why this works well:**
- Before switch: GoToColor(green) → no green visible, robots still move (exploring)
- After switch: green appears, robots perceive it with p=1.0, go to Stop
- S1 has tiny escape probability so robots mostly stay stopped

**Why not perfect:** GoToColor without visible target may not move optimally. Score ~6191 suggests some inefficiency.

**Improvements:**
1. Make S1 escape probability even lower (e.g., 0.0001)
2. Try Exploration instead of GoToColor for pre-switch movement (ensures movement)
3. Try 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop
4. Keep the champion's structure but tune probabilities
5. Explore making the green detection more robust

The score is ~6191. With 20 robots and ~500 steps before switch + ~700 after, perfect would be 0. Current score of ~6191 means roughly 6191/(20*700)≈0.44 robots moving per step after switch, or equivalent waste before.

The issue: before switch, GoToColor without green target might sometimes stop robots. Using Exploration (rwm) guarantees movement.

Let me focus on:
- Exploration before switch → Stop after green detected
- Very low escape probability from Stop

- S0: GoToColor(green), two transitions to S1:
  - C=7 (perceive green, p=1.0) → Stop
  - C=5 (random, p=0.002) → Stop
- S1: Stop, one transition back to S0:
  - C=5 (random, p=0.0002) → GoToColor

**Progress:** 10029 → 7887 → 6191 → 5169. Good improvement trend.

**Key insight:** The champion has a random drift transition (p=0.002) from S0→S1 which means ~0.2% chance per step of stopping even before green. This hurts pre-switch performance. However, it may help if GoToColor(green) sometimes stops robots (no target visible).

**What's working:** GoToColor(green) before switch (robots move), green perception triggers stop after switch.

**What to tune:**
1. The random drift p=0.002 from S0 might be slightly too high - reduce it
2. The escape probability p=0.0002 from S1 might be slightly too high - reduce it
3. Try removing the random drift entirely (p=0 effectively means no transition)
4. Try Exploration (B=0) instead of GoToColor - guarantees movement when no green visible
5. Try extremely low escape from Stop

**Key question:** Does GoToColor(green) move robots when no green is visible? If not, we need Exploration before switch. The score of 5169 with 20 robots and ~700 post-switch steps suggests ~5169/(20*700)≈0.37 fraction of time robots are moving after switch. That's not terrible but still significant.

Let me focus on:
- Reducing both probabilities further
- Testing Exploration variant
- Testing 3-state machines with better structure

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Situation:** Champion score stuck at 5169.81 for two rounds. Need to think more carefully.

**Deep analysis of the score:**
- Run is 1200 steps. Switch happens at ~500 steps (uniform 400-600).
- Before switch (~500 steps): 20 robots should MOVE → GoToColor(green) when no green visible... what does it do? It likely wanders/explores since no target, so robots move. Good.
- After switch (~700 steps): green appears, robots perceive it → transition to Stop. 

**Why 5169?** 5169/(20 robots) = 258 steps per robot wasted. With 700 post-switch steps, that's 258/700 = 37% waste. This suggests robots are NOT stopping quickly after switch, OR they drift back to moving state.

**The escape probability p=0.0002 from Stop:** Over 700 steps, probability of escaping = 1-(1-0.0002)^700 ≈ 13%. So ~2-3 robots escape per run and move for remaining time. That's significant.

**Key issue:** After stopping, robots can randomly re-enter GoToColor state (p=0.0002/step). If they do, and green is still visible, they immediately transition back. But GoToColor moves them briefly before re-stopping. 

**Also:** The random p=0.002 transition from S0→S1 means before switch, ~0.2%/step chance of stopping prematurely. Over 500 steps, probability ≈ 63% of at least one spurious stop per robot. That's a lot of pre-switch stopping!

**Fix:** Remove or drastically reduce the random transition from S0. Only use green perception to trigger stop.

**Better design:**
- S0: GoToColor(green) → only transition on green perception (C=7, p=1.0)
- S1: Stop → essentially no escape (p very small like 0.00001)

Or use Exploration which definitely moves robots.

Let me also consider: maybe the issue is that not all robots can see the green wall. The range-and-bearing is 0.5m but the camera can see walls. Robots far from the green wall may not perceive it. A relay mechanism could help: if neighbors stop (many neighbors below threshold?), also stop.

**New idea - neighbor-based relay:**
- S0: Explore/GoToColor
- S0→S1: perceive green (C=7) OR many neighbors stopped (hard to detect directly)
- S1: Stop

Actually C=3 (neighbors above threshold) could detect clustering. But stopped robots near each other... Let me try a neighbor-count trigger as backup.

**Plan:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Champion with p0x1 reduced to 0.0001 (less random stopping before switch)
3. Champion with p0x1=0.0005, p1x0=0.00005
4. Single transition only (no random drift from S0)
5. Exploration + green detection
6. 3-state with neighbor relay
7. Various combinations

# ROUND 7/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Stuck at 5169.81 for three rounds.** Need a fundamentally different approach.

**Deep re-analysis:**

The champion: GoToColor(green) → Stop on green perception OR random p=0.002.

The score of 5169 with 20 robots over 1200 steps: if robots move perfectly before switch (500 steps × 0 penalty) and stop perfectly after (700 steps × 0 penalty), score = 0. But we get 5169.

**What's actually happening:**
- GoToColor(green) when no green visible: the robot behavior is undefined/wandering, but it DOES move (good for pre-switch).
- After switch: green wall lights up. Robots that can SEE the green wall transition to Stop immediately.
- BUT: robots near the opposite wall or blocked by other robots may NOT perceive green!
- The p=0.002 random transition helps robots that can't see green eventually stop, but it's slow.

**The real problem:** Robots that can't see the green wall keep moving after the switch.

**Solutions:**
1. **Neighbor-based propagation:** If many neighbors are stopped (hard to detect), also stop. But we can't directly detect if neighbors are stopped.
2. **Use C=3 (neighbors above threshold) as a relay:** If robots near green wall stop and cluster, others could detect high neighbor count and also stop.
3. **Increase random stop probability** to catch robots that can't see green - but this hurts pre-switch.
4. **Two-phase with timer:** Use random probability to eventually stop all robots.

**New strategy - neighbor relay:**
- S0: GoToColor(green) or Exploration
- S0→S1: perceive green (C=7) OR many neighbors (C=3, threshold low like 2-3) - if stopped robots cluster, moving robots detect them
- S1: Stop, very small escape

Actually the key insight: stopped robots near the green wall form a cluster. Moving robots approach and detect neighbors → stop too. This creates a cascade!

Let me try:
- S0: Exploration (moves freely)
- S0→S1: green perception (C=7, p=1.0) OR neighbors above threshold (C=3, threshold=3, high steepness)
- S1: Stop, escape p=0.00001

Also try with GoToColor(green) as S0 since it naturally moves toward green when visible.

**Another angle:** The random p=0.002 from S0 means after 500 steps, P(never randomly stopped) = (1-0.002)^500 ≈ 0.37. So 63% of robots randomly stop before switch ends, costing score. This is BAD. Remove it.

**Conclusion:** The random drift from S0 is harmful. Remove it. Only stop on green perception. But robots that can't see green need another trigger - use neighbor count as relay.

# ROUND 8/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Stuck at 5169.81 for 4 rounds.** The neighbor-relay approach didn't help. Need to think differently.

**Fundamental re-examination:**

Score = 5169.81. With 20 robots and 1200 steps:
- If switch at step 500: pre-switch cost if all moving = 0, post-switch cost if all stopped = 0. Perfect = 0.
- Actual 5169 = roughly 258 robot-steps wasted.

**What GoToColor(green) does when no green is visible:** Looking at the ARGoS documentation, GoToColor moves toward the nearest visible colored object. If NO object of that color is visible, the robot likely just moves forward (or wanders). So robots DO move before switch. Good.

**After switch:** Green wall appears. Robots need to perceive it. The camera range should cover the whole arena if the wall is large enough. But walls are at the boundary - robots in the CENTER may not perceive the green wall due to distance/angle limitations.

**The p=0.002 random transition:** This causes ~63% of robots to randomly stop during 500 pre-switch steps. Cost = sum of geometric distribution. Expected steps stopped before switch ≈ significant. This IS hurting us.

**But without it, robots that can't see green never stop after switch.** This is the tradeoff.

**Critical insight I've been missing:** After switch, the green wall is visible. GoToColor(green) will MOVE ROBOTS TOWARD the green wall! Once they're close enough, they perceive it and stop. But they're moving toward it (costing score). Meanwhile robots far away are moving toward green (costing score).

**Alternative:** After green appears, we want robots to STOP. But GoToColor moves them toward green first.

**Better architecture:**
- S0: Exploration (moving randomly) - costs 0 pre-switch
- S0→S1: on green perception → Stop immediately
- S1: Stop - costs 0 post-switch  
- S1→S0: tiny probability (but this costs post-switch score)

The issue: robots that never perceive green stay in Exploration (moving) post-switch.

**The relay problem:** We need robots that CAN'T see green to still stop. Options:
1. Random probability (current approach - hurts pre-switch)
2. Neighbor-count relay (didn't work well)
3. GoAwayColor(green) - moves AWAY from green, might help spread robots to see it? No.
4. Use LED colors as relay: robots that stop change LED color, others detect the color change

**LED relay idea:**
- S0: Exploration, LED=off
- S0→S1: perceive green (C=7, color=2) → Stop with LED=green(actually LED colors are 0,4,5,6)
- S1: Stop, LED=4 (yellow) 
- S0→S2: perceive yellow LED from neighbors (C=7, color=4) → also stop
- Wait, C=7 checks for colored objects in camera. Do robot LEDs appear as colored objects? YES! Robots have LEDs visible to neighbors.

**This is the key insight!** Robots that stop near the green wall can signal neighbors via LED color. Neighbors perceive the LED color and also stop. This creates a cascade!

LED colors available: 0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

Use yellow (4) or magenta (5) or cyan (6) as the "stop signal":

- S0: Exploration, LED=0 (off)
- S0→S1: perceive green wall (C=7, l=2) → Stop, LED=yellow(4)
- S0→S1: perceive yellow LED (C=7, l=4) → Stop, LED=yellow(4)  
- S1: Stop, LED=4

This cascade means: robots near green wall stop and light up yellow → neighbors see yellow and stop → cascade spreads!

Let me design this carefully:

**3-state LED relay:**
- S0: Exploration, LED=0
- S0→S1: C=7, l=2 (green wall) p=1.0
- S0→S1: C=7, l=4 (yellow LED from stopped neighbor) p=1.0
- S1: Stop, LED=4 (yellow - signals to others)
- S1→S0: C=5, p=0.00001 (almost never escape)

**2-state LED relay:**
- S0: GoToColor(green) or Exploration, LED=4 (yellow - but then ALL robots signal from start, defeating purpose)

Actually for relay to work, only stopped robots should emit the signal. So:
- S0: Exploration, LED=0 (no signal)
- S1: Stop, LED=4 (yellow signal)
- Transition S0→S1 on green OR yellow neighbor perception

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Massive breakthrough!** Score dropped from 5169 to 247.88! The LED relay worked perfectly.

**Champion analysis:** Score 247.88.
- S0: GoToColor(green), LED=off
  - Transition 1: perceive green (C=7, l=2, p=1.0) → S1 (Stop)
  - Transition 2: perceive yellow LED (C=7, l=4, p=1.0) → S1 (Stop)
- S1: Stop, LED=yellow(4)
  - Transition: C=5, p=0 → never escapes (p=0 means stuck in Stop forever)

**Why it works so well:**
- Before switch: GoToColor(green) moves robots (no green visible, no yellow LEDs yet). Score ≈ 0.
- After switch: green wall appears → nearby robots see it → stop + emit yellow → neighbors see yellow → cascade stop signal propagates through swarm!
- p=0 escape from Stop means robots stay stopped permanently. 

**Why 247 not 0?**
- Some robots might stop briefly before switch due to... actually p=0 means NO escape, so once stopped they stay stopped.
- Wait - p=0 in C=5: probability 0 means it NEVER fires. So robots in S1 stay forever.
- The 247 cost must come from: time for cascade to propagate after switch (some robots keep moving briefly while cascade reaches them), OR GoToColor moving robots toward green wall after switch (they move before stopping).

**How to improve further:**
1. Add more relay colors - use magenta(5) or cyan(6) as additional relay signals
2. Use Exploration instead of GoToColor - GoToColor moves robots toward green after switch, costing score. Exploration moves randomly so might be faster to stop.
3. Make transition probability for yellow detection even more aggressive (already p=1.0)
4. Try 3-state to speed up cascade: Explore→GoToColor(green)→Stop
5. Try using multiple LED colors as relay to increase detection range
6. The key bottleneck: robots far from green wall take time to get the cascade. Can we make the cascade faster?

**Key improvement ideas:**
- Use Exploration (B=0) instead of GoToColor: robots that don't see green keep moving randomly, which might bring them closer to stopped robots faster
- Add neighbor-count (C=3) as additional stop trigger: if many neighbors stopped (clustering), also stop
- Try all three relay colors simultaneously
- Use magenta or cyan relay instead of yellow (no difference in principle, but worth testing)

**The escape probability p=0:** This is already optimal - robots never leave Stop. Keep this.

**GoToColor issue:** After switch, robots in GoToColor(green) state will MOVE TOWARD green wall before stopping. This costs score. Using Exploration instead means random movement (might be shorter path to a stopped neighbor).

Let me focus on:
1. Exact champion copy
2. Exploration variant with yellow relay
3. Exploration variant with magenta relay  
4. Exploration variant with cyan relay
5. GoToColor with multiple relay colors
6. 3-state variants
7. Adding neighbor-count as backup trigger

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion still at 247.88.** The LED relay variants didn't improve it. This is the final round (round 10), so we need to push hard.

**Deep analysis of 247.88:**
- With p=0 escape from Stop, robots never leave once stopped.
- GoToColor(green) before switch: robots move (good, score=0 pre-switch).
- After switch: green wall appears. Robots see green → stop + emit yellow. Neighbors see yellow → stop + emit yellow. Cascade.
- 247.88 / 20 robots = ~12.4 steps per robot wasted on average.
- This is the time for the cascade to reach all robots after the switch.

**Why Exploration variants didn't beat champion:**
- GoToColor(green) might actually spread robots toward the green wall, making them more likely to see it quickly. Or the random movement of exploration is similar.

**What can still be improved?**

1. **The 247 score**: ~12 steps per robot wasted. This is the propagation delay. The cascade travels at ~0.5m range per hop. If arena is ~2m wide, it needs ~4 hops = ~4-40 steps. 

2. **Speed up cascade**: Use BOTH green AND yellow as triggers, AND add neighbor-count (C=3) as backup for robots that are surrounded by stopped (but out-of-range-for-LED) robots.

3. **Two relay colors**: Use yellow AND magenta - stopped robots emit yellow, moving robots stop on either yellow OR magenta. But this doesn't help if only one color is used as signal.

4. **Key question**: Can we do better than 247? The theoretical minimum is the propagation delay of the cascade, which depends on robot density and arena size. With 20 robots in the arena, average spacing is reasonable. 

5. **Try**: Exploration with higher random walk rate to spread robots more evenly, making cascade faster.

6. **Try**: 3-state where S0=Exploration → S1=GoToColor(green) on any green/yellow signal → S2=Stop. This way robots move toward stopped cluster.

7. **The GoToColor behavior after switch**: When a robot in GoToColor(green) state sees the green wall, it moves TOWARD it (scoring penalty). If instead it's in Exploration, it moves randomly. Using GoAwayColor might be bad. But we could try: after seeing yellow (neighbor stopped), transition to Stop immediately without going to green first.

8. **Critical insight**: The champion already does: perceive yellow → stop. So the cascade IS happening. The 247 cost might mostly be from the very first robots that see green and MOVE TOWARD it before stopping (GoToColor moves them, costs a few steps), plus cascade propagation time.

9. **Alternative**: Use Exploration (random walk) instead of GoToColor. Exploration doesn't move toward green, so no wasted movement. But robots need to randomly encounter the green signal or a stopped neighbor.

10. **Best bet for improvement**: Try Exploration with multiple relay colors (yellow+magenta both trigger stop, stopped robots emit yellow). Or try a 3-state with attraction toward stopped cluster.

Let me focus on the most promising variants:
- Exploration + yellow relay (already tried, didn't beat champion)
- Champion with additional triggers (neighbor count)
- 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop with yellow relay
- GoToColor with magenta relay
- Adding C=3 neighbor threshold as additional stop trigger

**Final round strategy**: Mostly exploit champion topology, try small variations.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
